A Core Calculus for Type-safe Product Lines of C Programs
Ferruccio Damiani, Daisuke Kimura, Luca Paolini, Makoto Tatsuta

Abstract
In this paper we: (1) propose Lightweight C (LC), namely a core calculus that formalizes a proper subset of the ANSI C without preprocessor directives; (2) define Colored LC (CLC), namely LC endowed with ANSI C preprocessor directives; and (3) define a type system for CLC that guarantees that all programs to be generated by the C preprocessor are well-typed C programs. We believe that the simple formalization provided by CLC could be useful also for teaching purposes. Stefano Berardi spent most of his academic career at the Department of Computer Science of the University of Turin, where he conducts outstanding research on the logical foundations of computer science and on type-based program analyses. Over the years, he taught many courses, from BSc courses on programming with C to PhD courses on program analysis. Therefore, this paper fully falls within Stefano Berardi’s research and teaching activities.



To Stefano Berardi on the occasion of his
2^{6}
th birthday.

1
Introduction

A
Software Product Line (SPL)

is a family of similar programs, called
variants
, with well-documented commonalities and differences, generated
from a common code base
[
10
,
26
,
3
]
.
Each variant of an SPL can be identified by a set of
features
, called a
product
, where a feature is a name associated to a program functionality.

In the annotative approach to SPL implementation, some code fragments of the code base are associated with propositional formulas over features. Then, the
variant associated to a given product is generated by removing from the code base the code fragments that are annotated by a propositional formula that evaluates to false when the features in the product assume value true and the remaining features assume value false. The #define and #if directives of the C preprocessor
[
7
]
are widely used to write SPLs of C programs according to the annotative approach.

Contribution of the Paper

The contribution of this paper is twofold:

1.

we propose
Lightweight C (LC)
, a core calculus for C programs which models syntax and typing of a subset of C; and

2.

we propose
Colored LC (CLC)
, a core calculus for annotation-based SPLs of LC programs together with a family-based
type checking system which guarantees that
all the variants of a well-typed CLC SPL are well typed LC programs.

Although toolchains including a type checker for C code with preprocessor directives are available in the literature
[
19
,
18
]
,
we are not aware of any formalization of type checking of SPLs for a core calculus that is a proper subset of C.
We believe that the simple formalization provided by CLC could be useful also for teaching purposes.

Stefano Berardi spent most of his academic career at the Department of Computer Science of the University of Turin, where he conducts outstanding research on the logical foundations of computer science and on type-based program analyses, inspiring and motivating students and young researchers (including – in chronological order – the first author and the third author of this paper) and collaborated with several researchers (including – in reverse chronological order – the second, the fourth and the first author of this paper). Over the years, he taught many courses, from BSc courses on programming with C
to PhD courses on program analysis. Therefore, this paper fully falls within Stefano Berardi’s research and teaching activities.

Organization of the Paper

Section

2
introduces some background and discusses some related work on SPLs.
Section

3
illustrates the annotative approach to the implementation of SPLs of C programs by an example, that will be used as a running example through the paper;
Section

4
presents the syntax and the typing of LC;
Section

5
presents CLC and a family-based type system for CLC SPLs;

Section

6
shows that
the type checking system guarantees that all
all the variants of a well-typed CLC SPL are well typed LC programs; and

Section

7
outlines some possible directions for future work.

2
Background and Related Work

2.1
Background

An SPL can be structured into: (i) a

feature model (FM)
describing the variants in
terms of
features
, where each feature is a name representing an abstract description of functionality and each variant is identified by a set of features, called a
product
;
(ii) a
code base (CB)
comprising language dependent
reusable code artifacts that are used to build the variants; and (iii)
configuration knowledge (CK)
connecting feature model and artifact base by specifying
how, given a product, the corresponding variant can be derived from the code artifacts—thus inducing a mapping from products to variants, called the
variant generator (VG)
of the SPL.

Several approach to representation of FMs and to the implementation of SPLs (see, e.g.,
[
5
,
28
]
) have been proposed in the literature. In this paper we consider the
propositional representation
of FMs and the
annotative approach
to SPL implementation.

Definition 2.1

(FM in propositional representation)
.

An FM
\Phi

is a pair
(\mathcal{F},\phi)

where

\mathcal{F}
is a (finite)
set of features and

\phi
is a
propositional formula over
\mathcal{F}
(i.e., whose variables

F
are elements of
\mathcal{F}
), with the following syntax:

{\phi\;\,::=\;\,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{F}}}}}}\;\mid\;\,!\,\phi\;\mid\;\phi\,\&\&\,\phi\;\mid\;\phi\,||\phi\,\;\mid\;0\;\mid\;1

The products of an FM are the set of features

p\subseteq\mathcal{F}
such that
\phi
is
satisfied by assigning value 1 to the variables
F
in
p

and 0 to the variables in
\mathcal{F}\setminus p
.

Remark 2.2

(On the syntax used for the proposition formulas)
.

In
2.1
we have introduced propositional formulas over features by using logical operators and constants in the syntax of the C programming language, which is also the
syntax used in the conditions in the
#if
preprocessor directives. Namely:
\&\&
for conjunction,

||
for disjunction,
!
for negation, 0 for false, and 1 for true.

Notation 2.3

(A couple of abbreviation for propositional formulas)
.

Sometimes, to improved readability, we will use the following abbreviations:

•

\phi_{1}\Rightarrow\phi_{2}
as short for
!\phi_{1}\,||\,\phi_{2}
; and

•

\phi_{1}\Leftrightarrow\phi_{2}
as short for
(\phi_{1}\Rightarrow\phi_{2})\,\&\&\,(\phi_{2}\Rightarrow\phi_{1})
.

According to the annotative approach to SPL implementation:

•

the CB is a single program (possibly divided into different files) that contains (at least) all the code that occurs in any variant;

•

the CK is a mapping that annotates fragments of code in the CB with propositional expressions over features; and

•

the VG takes a product
p
and removes from the CB all the fragments that are annotated with a feature expression that evaluates to 0 when assigning value 1 to the variables
F
in
p

and 0 to the variables in
\mathcal{F}\setminus p
.

The pre-processor of C program language, with its directives
#define
and
#if
, is a prominent incarnation of the annotative approach to SPL implementation.
Many well-know software distributions
[
30
]
(e.g.,
apache,
berkeley db,
linux,
php,
python,
sqlite,
vim,
etc.)
can be understood as annotative implementations of an SPLs of C programs
[
20
]
. These, SPLs often have a huge number of products.
For instance, the Gentoo
[
13
]
source-based Linux
distribution consists of many highly-configurable packages;
the March 1st 2019 version of the Gentoo distribution comprises
671617 features spread across 36197 feature models, and thus up to
2^{671617}
products
[
22
]
. A number of questions, like the following one, naturally arise:

How can we ensure that the (up to)
2^{671617}
variants are well-typed C programs?

Clearly, even for SPLs with fewer features (e.g., one FM with 64 features, thus up to
2^{64}
products) generating and type checking each variant in isolation is unfeasible.

Several approaches to analysis of SPLs have been proposed in the literature (surveyed, e.g., in
[
31
]
). Among these approaches,
family-based approaches
can be characterized as follows (cf. Definition 4.1 in
[
31
]
):

An analysis of an SPL is family based if it: (a) operates only on the CB; and (b) incorporates the knowledge
in the FM and CK.

The key idea of family-based approaches is to analyze the code base and the feature model together, to check whether
some intended properties hold for all variants, aiming at the feasibility of implementing the analyses.

2.2
Related Work

We focus this discussion of related research on two aspects of our work:
core languages for C and core calculi for the annotative approach to SPL implementation.

On Core Languages for C

Albeit endowed with few keywords, C is a rather subtle language that few programmers know in detail because their wide room for undefined behavior
[
14
]
.
A complete formalization of the semantics for the
C11
language has been provided by Chucky Ellison in his Ph.D. thesis
[
11
]
(see also
[
12
]
);
the thesis surveys many attempts to provide a formal semantics for C.

A number of core languages for C have been proposed in literature, e.g.: Featherweight C
[
29
]
, a “simple C-like language”
[
16
]
, and Clight
[
6
]
.
We designed LC aiming for a calculus that has a relationship to C somewhat similar to that of lambda-calculus
[
9
]
(see also
[
4
]
) or PCF
[
25
]
with programming languages such as ML
or Haskell, and that of Featherweight Java (FJ)
[
15
]
with Java. None of the core languages for C mentioned above satisfy this requirement: Featherweight C
[
29
]
and the “simple C-like language” are on the one hand too small (they do not model structures) and on the other hand too large (they model functions’ local variables and statements); while Clight is definitely too big.

It is worth observing that there is a substantial difference between the relations: (1) of the lambda-calculus or PCF with ML
or Haskell; and (2) of FJ with Java. Namely, the lambda-calculus and PCF are Turing complete, while FJ has not
beeind designed with the goal of being (and, to the best of our knowledge, it is not) Turing complete.
LC is (assuming integers of arbitrary size) Turing-complete and it
goes (by including integers, imperative assignment of values to struct-members, conditional-expressions, expression sequences, operators, and dynamic memory allocation/deallocation on the heap) a bit — we believe not too much — beyond the spirit of FJ, hence the name we choose.

On Core Calculi for the Annotative Approach to SPL Implementation

Although:

•

a formal account of family-based type checking of annotation-based SPLs of Java programs has been proposed
by means of the minimal core calculus Colored Featherweight Java (CFJ)
[
17
]
, which formalizes family-based type checking of SPLs of
(cast-free) Featherweight Java (FJ)
[
15
]
programs; and

•

TyeChef,
1
1

1

https://github.com/ckaestne/TypeChef
an infrastructure which includes a type checker for SPLs of C programs that implements variability with the
#define
and
#ifdef
directives of the
C preprocessor, has been proposed
[
19
]
, formalized
[
18
]
on top of a core calculus for module systems
[
8
]
, and used as basis for implementing a number of SPL analyses (see, e.g.,
[
21
,
23
,
27
]
);

we are not aware of any formalization of family-based type checking for annotation-based SPLs on top of a core language that is a proper subset of C.
Our formalization of the annotative approach to the implementation of SPL of C programs is inspired by the formalization
introduced in Kastner et al.’s paper on CFJ
[
17
]
.
We refer to that paper for a discussion of related work on core calculi for the annotative approach to SPL implementation, and to a couple of survey papers for broader discussions about SPL implementation approaches
[
28
]
and analysis strategies for SPLs
[
31
]
.
A key difference between CLC and CFJ is that LC models imperative variable (struct-member) assignment and dynamic memory allocation/deallocation on the heap; therefore CLC provides a suitable basis for investigating family-based analyses of SPLs of C programs for properties like, e.g., heap-safety.

3
Running Example

As a running example we consider an SPL of FIFO queues of integers, implemented by linked lists, that we call the
Queue SPL
.
All variants of the Queue SPL define a struct of name
fifo
, which has a member
first
of type
struct

node
*

containing the (pointer to the first node of the) list containing the elements of the queue (
NULL
if the queue is empty),
and some functions.

The Queue SPL has three features and six products (and, therefore, six variants). The features are:

•

LAST
, specifying that the struct of name
fifo

has a member
last
of type
struct

node
*

containing the pointer to the last element of the list containing the elements of the queue (or
NULL
, if the queue is empty);

•

OCCURS
, specifying that there is a function
int

occurs
(
struct

node
*

list
,

int

element
)
that checks whether the given
list
contains an occurrence of the given
element
; and

•

INTERVAL
, specifying that there is a function
int

occurs
(
struct

node
*

list
,

int

start
,

int

end
,

int

element
)
that checks whether the given
list
contains the given element in a position between the given positions
start
and
end
(the fist element of the list is position 0).

The six variants (C programs) of the Queue SPL are identified by the six products described by the FM

\Phi_{\mathit{queue}}=({\mathcal{F}}_{\mathit{queue}},\phi_{\mathit{queue}})
, where:

•

{{{{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathit{queue}}=\{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{LAST}}}}}},\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{OCCURS}}}}}},\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{INTERVAL}}}}}}\}

Remark 3.1

(A couple of annotative C SPL programming patterns)
.

The code in lines 22-30 of
Figure

5
is an instance of the following annotative C SPL programming pattern.

Pattern 1

To provide
n\geq 2

alternative versions of an expression (to be included in the code of different variants), proceed as follows. First, provide the sequence
(e_{1},...,e_{n})
of the
n

expressions. Then, annotate the comma between
e_{j}
and
e_{j+1}

(1\leq j\leq n-1)
with a propositional formula that evaluates always to 0 (e.g., by 0 itself). Finally,
annotate each expression
e_{i}

(1\leq i\leq n)
with a suitable propositional formula
\phi_{i}
over features; i.e., the formulas
\phi_{i}
must be
(modulo the FM) pairwise mutually exclusive and (modulo the FM) at least one of them must be always satisfied.

Instead of annotating the comma in line 26 with “0”, we have annotated it with the formula “LAST && !LAST”.
This makes the code in lines 22-30 also an instance of the following more general annotative C SPL programming pattern:

Pattern 2

Consider a sequence
(e_{1},...,e_{n})
of
n\geq 2

expressions. Annotate each expression
e_{i}

(1\leq i\leq n)
with a propositional formula
\phi_{i}
over features
such that (modulo the FM) at least one of the
\phi_{i}
’ is always satisfied.
Annotate the
n-1
commas separating the
n
expressions in the sequence
(e_{1},...,e_{n})
with the
n-1
formulas
\psi_{i}

(1\leq i\leq n-1)
(respectively), where
\psi_{i}
is defined as:

\psi_{i}=\phi_{i}\;\&\&\;(||_{i<j<n}\phi_{j})=\phi_{i}\;\&\&\;(\phi_{i+1}\;||\cdots||\;\phi_{n-1})\;.

This ensures that, whenever
m
expressions
(m<n)
of the
e_{i}

(1\leq i\leq n)
are removed, then
m
commas are removed in such a way the
remaining code forms a well-formed sequence of expressions.

4
Lightweight C

In this section we introduce
Lightweight C (LC)
, a core calculus for C programs.

4.1
LC Syntax

The abstract syntax of LC is given in
Figure

7
where, following
[
15
,
24
]
, we adopt the overline notation
\overline{\cdot}

to denote (possibly empty) sequences. We use the unary function
\#(\cdot)
do denote the length of a sequence.
In particular, “
{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}}

{{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}}\,\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}}

, respectively. The empty sequence is denoted by “
\bullet
” and a non-empty sequence of expressions is denoted
{\tilde{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}}
.
We assume that the elements of list of named entities (i.e., struct definitions, struct member declarations, function definitions, function formal parameter declarations) have different names.

2
2

2

(

(

which has return type “
void
\ast
” (instead of “
void
”) and always returns
\mathtt{NULL}
.

Remark 4.1

(On the syntax of LC and the syntax of ANSI C)
.

The syntax in
Figure

7
is subset of the syntax defined in the C24 Standard. In particular, we note that in accord to Sect.6.5.18 (Comma Operator) of
[
2
]
non-empty parenthesized expression-sequences (
{\widetilde{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}}
) can be supplied as a single argument in a function call; e.g., given a funtion with prototype “
int
f
(
int
x
);
” we can
call it as
“
f
((2,4))
”, which is equivalent to “
f
(4)
’’.
We remind that references to struct-names are allowed without requiring that their definitions be available before
3
3

3

This is possible because,
in
[
2
, Sect.6.2.5]
is explained that “All pointers to structure types shall have
the same representation and alignment requirements as each other.” while, on the other hand, different kinds of pointers may have distinct width and/or alignment properties.
,
thus allowing recursive and mutual recursive types definitions.

Figure 7:
LC abstract syntax, where
s
ranged over struct names,
m
over struct member names,
f
over function names,
x
over function formal parameter names, n over interger values,
uop
over unary operators and
bop
over binary operators.

A program consists of a sequence of struct declarations followed by a sequence of function declarations which always contains a function
main

which takes no arguments and returns an integer.

To simplify the presentation, in what follows we consider, in the obvious way, a struct definition
SD
as a mapping from each member name
m

to member declaration
T

m
, a sequence of struct definitions
{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}}
as a mapping from each struct name
s
to the struct definition
SD
, a sequence of function definitions
{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}}
as a mapping from a function name
f
to function definition
FD
, and a program
Prg
as the union of the mappings
{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}}
and
{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}}
.

We write “
Prg

sane
” to mean that the program
{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}=\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}}\;\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}}
satisfies the following sanity conditions:
(1)
for every struct name
s
appearing everywhere in

we have
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\in\mathsf{dom}(\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}})
;
(2)
for every function name
f
appearing everywhere in
{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}}
we have
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{f}}}}}}\in\mathsf{dom}(\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}})

(3)
{{{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{main}}}}}})=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{int}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{main}}}()\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{return}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}};\textbraceright}}}

4.2
LC Typing

Unary and binary operators are listed in

8
, together with their types and a brief description. Given a (unary or binary) operator

, we write

(

) to denote its type.

Figure 8:
LC operators.

Following the convention in
[
24
]
, a
type environment

\Gamma
is a finite mapping from function formal parameter names to types, written “
{{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}}:\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}}
” which (as usual) is short for
“
{{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}_{1}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1},...,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}_{n}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{n}
” with
n\geq 0
. In
Figure

9
, we define three kinds of typing judgements: we write
{\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
to denote that
Prg
is well typed; we write
{\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
to denote that functions defined in
{\mathsf{dom}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}})
are well typed; and, we write
{{\Gamma\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}
to denote that the expression
e
has type
T
under the assumptions in the environment
\Gamma
.
The
symbol
\leq
denotes the subtyping relation,
which is the reflexive closure of the relation defined by

void
\ast

\leq

struct
s
\ast
, for all struct
s
defined in the program.
We write
{{\mathsf{max}_{\leq}\{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1},\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{2}\}
to denote the greatest between types
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}
and
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{2}
.

5
Colored Lightweight C

In this section we introduce
Colored LC (CLC)
, a core calculus for SPLs of (L)C programs.
To represent the annotations, we adopt the approach proposed for Colored Featherweight Java (CFJ)
[
17
]
.

5.1
CLC Syntax

The C preprocessor allows to remove arbitrary code fragments (even fragments starting in the body of a function and ending in the body of another function), making the process prone to syntactical errors.
As in CFJ
[
17
]
, in
CLC, we adopt a neat discipline on what can be removed. Namely, we allow
programmers to remove some of the
highlighted in gray
elements in Figure
10
:
any struct definition,
any member of a struct, any function definition, any formal parameter of a function definition, any actual parameter of a function application, and any expression in an expression sequence.
It is worth to note that in the concrete syntax the list of expressions include commas that can be annotated, but in the abstract one we neglect their presence:
they can be faced in accord to Pattern 2 of (cf. Remark
3.1
) and they can be managed uniformly as list-elements.

Prg

::=

{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}}

{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}}

Expression

Figure 10:
LC syntax (the same as in Figure
7
) where the parts
highlighted in gray
are those that can be annotated.

We model code annotations in the CB by using an
annotation table
, that maps each occurrence of an annotable code fragment (identified by its location, e.g.,
in the text or in the syntax tree, of the CB)
4
4

4

So, distinct occurrences of a same code fragment can have different annotations.
to a propositional formula over features specifying whether
the occurrence of the code fragment must be kept when generating variants. Therefore a CLC program, that represents an SPL of LC programs, is a triple
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
where:

•

\Phi
is an FM (see
2.1
),

•

Prg
is an LC program, which represents the CB, and

•

at
is an annotation table, which expresses CK.

To simplify the
formalization, in what follows we always assume a fixed SPL
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
.

We use the metavariables
a
and
b
to refer to arbitrary annotable code fragments.
For every annotable fragment
a
of the CB and for every product
p
of the FM, the propositional formula
\textsc{at}(a)
is such that if
p\models\textsc{at}(a)
(where
p
is used as the interpretation which maps to
1
the features in
p
and maps to
0
the other features) then the code fragment
a
must be kept when generating the variant for
p
, otherwise it must be removed. The fragments without annotation are implicitly assumed to be annotated by the constant
1
(i.e., to be present in all variants).

Remark 5.1

(On the CLC annotation table and the #if preprocessor directives)
.

It is straightforward to see that the CK information specified by the annotation table can be implemented by the
#if
preprocessor directives (cf.
Section

3
).

Remark 5.2

(Syntactic sugar for the CLC annotation table)
.

In the examples, we assume that it is also possible to annotate any occurrence of a binary logic operator together with either its left or its right argument; i.e., each occurrence of an expression “
e
1

bop

e
2
”, where
bop
is a binary logic operator, may be either
left-annotated
as
“
e
1

bop

e
2
” or
right-annotated
as “
e
{}_{1}\!

bop

e
2
”. It is worth observing that this kind of annotations:

•

can be expressed by using the
#if
preprocessor directives, as done in the code in lines 40-42 and 44-46 in
Figure

5
; and

•

can be formalized by introducing some complexity in the definition of the annotation table.

To keep the presentation simpler, we have preferred not to formalize left- and right-annotations of binary logic operators and to consider:

•

“
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1}\ \textsf{bop}\ \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{2}
” with
{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1}\ \textsf{bop})=\psi
as syntactic sugar for the expression sequence “
{{{(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1}\ \textsf{bop}\ \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{2},\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{2})

with
{{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1}\ \textsf{bop}\ \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{2})=\psi
and
{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{2})=!\psi
; and

•

“
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1}\ \textsf{bop}\ \lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{2}

Notation 5.4

(Some abbreviation for propositional formulas involving the annotation table)
.

•

We write “
\textsc{at}(\bar{a})\odot\textsc{at}(\bar{b})
”, with
\odot\in\{\Rightarrow,\Leftrightarrow\}
(see Notation
2.3
), as short for

“
(\textsc{at}(a_{1})\odot\textsc{at}(b_{1}))\,\&\&\,\cdots\,\&\&\,(\textsc{at}(a_{n})\odot\textsc{at}(b_{n}))
”

thus also stating that the sequences
\bar{a}
and
\bar{b}
have the same length
n
.

•

We write “
{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}})
” as short for

–

“1”, if
{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}\in\{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{int}}}}}},\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{void}}}}}}\ast\}
, which states that types
int
and
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{void}}}}}}\ast

are always present;
and

–

“
{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}))}}}
”, if

{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{struct}}}}}}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\ast
, which states that
the struct
s
is present.

•

We write
“
{\exists(\tilde{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}})
” as short for
“
{{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1})\,||\,\cdots\,||\,\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{n})
”, which states that there is at least one
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{i}
in
{\tilde{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}}
such that
{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{i})
is true;
i.e., that
there is at least an expression of
{\tilde{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}}
that is not removed in the generation of some variant.

•

We write
“
{\texttt{neverLast}(k,\bar{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}})
” as short for “
{{{!\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{k})\,||\,\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{k+1})\,||\,\ldots\,||\,\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{n})
, which states that
in every variant
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{k}
is never the last (not suppressed) expression of
{\bar{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}}
, viz., if it is not removed in the generation of a variant then at least one subsequent espression is not removed in the generation of that variant.

5.2
CLC Typing

In order to guarantee that all the variants of a CLC SPL are well-typed LC programs
we define a family-based type system for CLC SPLs.

An
annotated type environment

\Delta
is a finite mapping from variables to pairs of a type and a proposition formula over features, written

{{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}}:\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}}\overset{\text{with }\overline{\psi}}{\;}
which (as usual) is short for

{{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}_{1}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}\overset{\text{with }\psi_{1}}{\;},...,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}_{n}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{n}\overset{\text{with }\psi_{n}}{\;}
with
n\geq 0
.
The family-based typing judgements are:

•

{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
, to be read “the SPL
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
with
\Phi=(\mathcal{F},\phi)
is well typed”;

•

{\theta\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
, to be read “
SD
is well typed under the configuration assumption
5
5

5

We call configuration assumption the propositional formulas that we use in the family-based typing judgements.

\theta
”;

•

{\theta\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
, to be read “
FD
is well typed under the configuration assumption
\theta
”; and

•

{{\theta;\Delta\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}
, to be read “expression
e
has type
T
under the configuration assumption
\theta
and the assumptions in the annotated type environment
\Delta
”.

The family-based typing rules are given in
Figure

11
, where we use the abbreviations introduced in Notation
5.4
and
in the premises of rule
FT-prg
we write “
{{\phi\,\&\&\,\textsc{at}(\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}})\vdash\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
”
as abbreviation for “
{{\phi\,\&\&\,\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}_{1})\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}_{1}\;\textsc{ok}

\cdots

{{\phi\,\&\&\,\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}_{n})\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}_{n}\;\textsc{ok}
”
where
n\geq 0
is length of the sequence
{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{SD}}}}}}}
,
and similarly for “
{{\phi\,\&\&\,\textsc{at}(\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}})\vdash\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{FD}}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
”.
As usual,
\theta\models\theta^{\prime}
means that the propositional formula
\theta\Rightarrow\theta^{\prime}
is valid (i.e., it is satisfied by all assignments of truth values to its variables).

SPL family-based typing

\phi

{\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}

(

)

sane
at
(

) =
1

Figure 11:
Family-based typing of a CLC SPL
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
with
\Phi=(\mathcal{F},\phi)
; the extensions w.r.t. LC typing (in
Figure

9
) are
highlighted in grey.

The CLC family-based type system (which types a CLC SPL as whole) checks some
presence-in-variant
conditions in addition to the standard checks done by the LC type system (which types a single LC program).
For instance, we have to express condition like “whenever code fragment
a
is present (in a variant), then also code fragment
b
is present (in the same variant)”,
or in other words “whenever the annotation of
a
evaluates to 1 (modulo a product
p
) then also the annotation of
b
evaluates to 1 (modulo the same product
p
)”.

Namely, each family-based typing rule is obtained from an LC typing rule (cf.
Figure

9
) by adding checks (
highlighted in grey
) involving the FM and CK (i.e., the annotation table) —
in particular each family-based typing rule
FT-¡name¿
is obtained from the typing rule
T-¡name¿
.

The extra-conditions checked by CLC SPL family-based typing follow.

P1

(in rule
FT-prg
) the function
main
must be present.

S1

(in rule
FT-struct
)
If a member
T

m
of
struct

s
is present then its type
T
is present.

F1

(in rule
FT-fun
) If function definition
T
0

f
is present then its return type
T
0
is present.

F2

(in rule
FT-fun
) If an actual parameter
T

x
(of a present function) is present then its type
T
is present.

E1

(in rule
FT-par
) If the occurrence of a function formal parameters
x
is present in the body of a (present) dunction then
declaration of
x
in the header of the function is present.

E2

(in rule
FT-app
) If a function call expression
f
(
{\bar{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}}
) is present then: (i) the function
f
is present
and (ii) the types and the number of expressions in the sequence of actual paremeter list
{\bar{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}}
match those of the formal parameter list in the definition of
f
.

E3

(in rules
FT-member
and
FT-assign
)
If an expression
e
->
m
is present then the structure
s
such that

{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{struct}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\ast
is the type of
e
is present and containts a member
m
.

E4

(in rule
FT-seq
) If an expression sequence
{{(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1},...,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{n})
is present then at least an expression of the sequence is present.

E5

(in rule
FT-seq
) If an expression
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{i}

(1\leq i\leq n)
in an sequence
{{(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{1},...,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{n})
has a type
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{i}
that is different
from the type
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{n}
of the last expression of the sequence, then for some
j\in\{i+1,...,n\}
the expression
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{j}
is present (so
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}_{i}
cannot be the last included in the list in any variant).

E6

(in rule
FT-malloc
) If an expression
\mathtt{MALLOC}
(
struct
s
) is present then the struct
s
is present.

Let
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
be an SPL. If
p
is a valid product then
{\lsem\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\rsem_{p}

is a program in accord
with the grammar of Figure
7
(possibly not well-typed).

Example 5.5

(Family-based typing of the Queue SPL)
.

The Queue SPL given in Example
5.3
is a well-typed CLC SPL.

5.3
Variant Generation

In order to generate program variants, we have to define the variant generator VG for CLC SPLs.
Given product
p
, the generator VG descends the syntactic tree of the
Prg
(viz. the CB of the SPL)
and removes all code fragments annoted by the annotation table
at
with a formula
\phi
which evaluates to
0
under the interpretation identified by
p
.

Given a code base
CB
and a product
p
, we denote
\lsem\texttt{CB}\rsem_{p}
the corresponding variant.
The definition of the variant generator is given in Fig.
12
, it is defined by means of two mutual recursive defined functions:

\lsem\_\rsem_{p}
suppress the elements of list annoted with formulas evaluated in false by
p
,
while
\llangle\_\rrangle_{p}
descends the syntax tree and apply
\lsem\_\rsem_{p}
to annotated sequences.

\begin{array}[]{rcl<{\hspace{3.8mm}\scalebox{.8}{(\textsc{vgList})}}}\hline\cr\\
\lsem a_{1}\ldots a_{n}\rsem_{p}&:=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bullet&\text{if }n==0\\
\llangle a_{1}\rrangle\lsem a_{2}\ldots a_{n}\rsem_{p}&\text{if }p\models\textsc{at}(a_{1}),n\geq 1\\
\lsem a_{2}\ldots a_{n}\rsem_{p}&\text{if }p\not\models\textsc{at}(a_{1}),n\geq 1\\
\end{array}\right.\hskip 10.81204pt\\
\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

{{{{{{{{{{{{{{{{{\begin{array}[t]{rcl<{\hspace{\fill}\scalebox{.8}{(\textsc{vg\therowVG})\stepcounter{rowVG}}}}\\
\llangle\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{struct}}}{\@listingGroup{}{$\,$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\{\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}}\,\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{m}}}}}}};\};\rrangle_{p}&:=&\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{struct}}}{\@listingGroup{}{$\,$}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\{\ \lsem\overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}}\ \overline{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{m}}}}}}};\rsem_{p}\};\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{.8}{({vg0\\

Example 5.6

(The variants of the Queue SPL)
.

Consider the well-typed Queue SPL given in Example
5.3
. Its variants, generated by applying the VG to the annotated code base illustrated in
Figure

6
, are the six well-typed LC programs given in Example
4.3
.

6
Properties

An SPL of programs written in a given programing language PL is
type safe
if all its variants are well-typed programs
according to the type system of PL.
In this section we prove
type safety
for CLC, i.e.,
well-typed CLC SPLs are type safe.
We first prove (in
Section

6.1
) that the code base of a well-typed CLC SPL is a well-typed LC program and some auxiliary lemmas; then
(in
Section

6.2
) we prove some basic guaranntess on variant generation and the type-safety result.

6.1
The Code Base of a Well-typed CLC SPL is a Well-typed LC Program and Some Auxiliary Lemmas

Theorem 6.1

(The code base of a well-typed CLC SPL is a well-typed LC program)
.

Consider a CLC SPL
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
with
\Phi=(\mathcal{F},\phi)
.
If
{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
then
{\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
.

Proof.

First recall that the LC typing rules, in
Figure

9
, can be otained from the family-based typing rules, in
Figure

11
, by ignoring
the parts
highlighted in grey.
Namely, by:
(i) neglecting the premises about the typing of structures in
FT-prg
(indeed, LC just requires the sanity condition about structures); and
(ii) neglecting the premises that involve
\models
in the other rules (i.e. checks about annotations).
Then, the proof is straightforward by induction on the derivation
\mathcal{D}
proving
{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
.
∎

We now present some auxiliary lemmas.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the derivation proving
{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
.
It is worth to remark that the derivation
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
of
{\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
built in the proof of

6.1

is, roughly speaking, obtained from
\mathcal{D}
by removing all gray-identified (in accord to
Figure

11
) parts of its rules and then replacing each rule
FT-¡name¿
with rule
T-¡name¿
.

Lemma 6.2

(On subtyping an annotations in family-based typing)
.

Consider a CLC SPL
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
and two types

{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{0},\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}
ocurring in
Prg
.
If
{\theta\models\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1})
and
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{0}\leq\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}
then
{\theta\models\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{0})
.

Proof.

First observe that if
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{0}\leq\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}
then the possible cases are as follows:
(i) if
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{0}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{int}}}}}}
then
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{int}}}}}}
;
(ii) if
{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{0}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{struct}}}}}}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\ast
for some
struct
s
, then
{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{structs}}}}}}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\ast
;
and, (iii) if
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{0}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{void}}}}}}\ast
then, either
{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{void}}}}}}\ast
or
{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}_{1}=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{struct}}}}}}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{s}}}}}}\ast
for some struct. In all cases the proof is straightforward, in accord to Notation
5.4
.
∎

Let
J
and
J^{\prime}
be two judgment occurrences of the derivation
\mathcal{D}
of
{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
.
We say that a statement
J^{\prime}
is a descendant of
J
(
J
is an ascendant of
J^{\prime}
) whenever
J^{\prime}
occurs in the (sub)derivation of
J
.

Lemma 6.3

(On the formulas on the left of family-based typing judgment)
.

Consider a CLC SPL
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
with
\Phi=(\mathcal{F},\phi)
.
Let
J
and
J^{\prime}
be two judgment
occurrences in the derivation of
{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
such that
J^{\prime}
is a descendant of
J
.
If
\theta_{J}
is the leftmost formula of a judgment
J
and
\theta^{\prime}_{J}
is the leftmost formula of
J^{\prime}

then
\theta^{\prime}_{J}\models\theta_{J}
.

Proof.

The proof is done by induction on the derivation of
{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}
. Straighforwardly,
each leftmost formula
\theta^{\prime}
in premises is: (i) the same leftmost formula
\theta

of the judgment in the conclusion of the rule; or, (ii) the conjunction of
\theta
with another formula.
Therefore, if
\theta_{J}
is the leftmost formula of a judgment
J
having
J^{\prime}
as descendant (leftmost annotated
\theta^{\prime}_{J}
), then
\theta^{\prime}_{J}\models\theta_{J}
.
∎

Lemma 6.4

(On family-based typing of expressions)
.

Consider a CLC SPL
{(\Phi,\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}},\textsc{at})
with
\Phi=(\mathcal{F},\phi)
.
If
{{\theta;\Delta\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}
is included in
a derivation
{\mathcal{D}^{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}}
proving
{\phi\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}\;\textsc{ok}

then
{\theta\models\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}})
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{D}
be a sub-derivation proving
{{\theta;\Delta\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}
then, the proof follows by induction on the last rule used in
\mathcal{D}
.
Note that
\mathcal{D}
is a sub-derivation included in
{\mathcal{D}^{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}}
by a unique rule
FT-fun
used immediately over the unique application of
FT-prg
, viz. the judgment
{{\theta;\Delta\vdash\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{e}}}}}}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}
is about a fragment of the body of a function
{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{f}}}}}}_{\mathcal{D}}
.
We call this unique parent rule application the
FT-fun
-parent of
\mathcal{D}
.

{{{\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{int}}}}}})=\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{void}}}}}}\ast)=\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers 1}}}

for some
T
x
.
Indeed,

is the with-labeling formula of a
{{{{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}}:\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}\overset{\text{ with }\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}})}{\;}
added to the type environment by the
FT-fun
-parent of

.
This parent rule contains, in its premises,

{{{\theta^{\prime}\models\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{x}}}}}})\Rightarrow\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{T}}}}}})
where
{{\theta^{\prime}=\phi\&\&\textsc{at}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}(\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\textrm{f}}}}}}))
,
because

is immediately over the rule

.
Since

. Thus

,
and we conclude

.

{\mathcal{D}^{\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\textrm{Prg}}}}}}}

follows.

. Thus,
